People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s probably not as bad as it sounds, but it’s been enough that I thought I should come in and ask about it.

I mean… I don’t want to overstate it, but these past couple weeks I’ve been waking up with this heavy, kind of hollow feeling. Like the shine’s gone out of everything, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ve even cried in my truck before opening the shop a couple times, which is… embarrassing to say. I’m not sure if that counts as depressed, but it hasn’t felt like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not fidgety exactly, but kind of restless in the sense that I kept needing to get things moving. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these feelings of restlessness or the heavy, hollow feeling tend to happen at a particular time of day?\n\nPatient: Mostly in the morning, I think. These past couple weeks, I’ll wake up and it’s just… there, this heavy, hollow feeling, like everything’s kind of lost its shine.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but a couple times it stuck with me enough that I cried in my truck before opening the shop. I’m not sure if that counts as depressed, but it hasn’t felt like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not fidgety exactly, but kind of restless in the sense that I kept needing to get things moving. I started lining up new commissions, messaging old customers, planning out a website, and sketching this whole Appalachian collection all at the same time.\n\nI told myself it was just being productive, you know, trying to be motivated… but looking back, it was a lot to take on at once, like I felt I had to do it all right then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these feelings of restlessness or the heavy, hollow feeling tend to happen at a particular time of day?\n\nPatient: Mostly in the morning, I think. These past couple weeks, I’ll wake up and it’s just… there, this heavy, hollow feeling, like everything’s kind of lost its shine.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but a couple times it stuck with me enough that I cried in my truck before opening the shop. Then I try to pull myself together and get on with the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been acting on impulses more than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in any big way, at least.\n\nI mean, I’ve probably been a little quicker to say yes to things or jump into plans than usual, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m out of control or anything. I haven’t really noticed myself doing anything that feels… reckless, I guess.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of restlessness and the heavy, hollow feeling tend to improve as the day goes on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… usually it’s worst right when I wake up. That heavy, hollow feeling is just kind of sitting there, like the shine’s gone out of everything.\n\nIt can ease up some once I get to the shop and start moving around, I guess. I don’t want to overstate it, but a couple mornings it was bad enough that I cried in my truck before opening up. Then I sort of try to shake it off and get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you, like noise or movement, even when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I just can’t function, but that’s honestly part of what brought me in.\n\nAt the bench, my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll start answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark on a piece, then hear something outside, and before I know it I’ve lost the whole work order. It’s embarrassing, because I’m usually pretty careful with that stuff. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mood changes quickly in response to small things that happen throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I wouldn’t say it’s, like, flipping back and forth all day over every little thing.\n\nI can get thrown off by something small, maybe more than I’d like to admit, but I usually try to keep it to myself and move on. It’s hard for me to tell if that’s a “mood change” exactly or just me being stressed, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been withdrawing from social activities or feeling like you're a burden to your friends and family lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic or like I’m trying to make people worry.\n\nIt’s more… I keep going over every client promise I didn’t follow through on, every job I feel like I messed up, and then I start telling myself I’m kind of a fraud. Like I embarrassed my family, especially after that award thing. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nSo I do pull back some, because I don’t really want to sit there and have everyone see that, or feel like they have to reassure me. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you interact with your friends and family since you started experiencing these difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I’ve been trying not to make it everybody else’s problem, but my family’s been checking in on me more than usual. I think they noticed I’m not really keeping up with my normal routines the way I used to.\n\nWith friends and customers, I’ve kind of pulled back too. I missed a big custom delivery, which was… pretty humiliating, honestly. And my shop records got so messy that I had to stop taking new work for now, just until I get things straightened out.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of change you mean, but it’s definitely affected how available I am to people.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently found yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I might talk over someone once in a while if I’m nervous or trying to explain myself, but I don’t think I’ve been blurting things out or cutting people off a lot.\n\nIf someone else has noticed it, I guess I’d want to know… but from my side, it doesn’t feel like a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking a lot more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not usually. I mean, I can talk a fair amount if it’s something I know well, like work stuff, but I don’t think I’m the person dominating every conversation or anything.\n\nAt least, nobody’s told me that. If that’s something people might not say to my face, I guess I’m not totally sure, but from what I’ve noticed, no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're dominating conversations when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I try to be pretty aware of that, especially with family. I’d feel bad if I was just taking over the whole conversation.\n\nMaybe sometimes I explain too much if I’m stressed or trying to make sure someone understands me, but I wouldn’t say I often feel like I’m dominating things. At least… not from my side. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or meeting deadlines at work due to your difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but yes, it has affected work.\n\nI’m usually pretty careful with custom jobs, but I missed a major delivery, which was… really embarrassing. And my shop records got so messy that I’ve had to stop taking new work for now, just until I can get it back under control.\n\nMy family’s been checking on me more too, because I guess I’ve gotten pretty far off my normal routines. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m trying to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your difficulties with focus, mood, and restlessness are causing you significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’d have to say yes, even if I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not like everything has completely fallen apart, but it has gotten in the way. I missed a major custom delivery, which was really not like me, and my shop records got so disorganized that I had to stop taking new work for now. That’s been pretty stressful.\n\nAnd my family’s been checking on me more, because they got worried about how far I’d slipped from my usual routines. So… yes, I guess it is causing problems day to day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Then I sort of try to shake it off and get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you, like noise or movement, even when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I just can’t function, but that’s honestly part of what brought me in.\n\nAt the bench, my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll start answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark on a piece, then hear something outside, and before I know it I’ve lost the whole work order. There have been ups and downs, sure, but if you mean the focus and mood problems being there for six months straight, I wouldn’t want to say yes if that’s not accurate. It feels more recent than that.
